Remark 2.6
.

The valued quivers for simply-laced finite types coincide with the infinite quivers in
[
30
, Section 2.1.3]
where the infinite quivers are denoted by
\Gamma
.

Definition 2.7

(cf.
[
18
, Definition 5.5]
)
.

(1)

We denote by
{\overline{\triangle}}
the quiver obtained from
{\widetilde{\triangle}}
by removing all horizontal arrows. We call
{\overline{\triangle}}
the
valued repetition quiver
of
\triangle
4
4

4

When we replace valued arrows with usual arrows, it is the usual repetition quiver
\widehat{\triangle}
(see
[
48
]
for non-simply-laced types).
.

(2)

A subset
\mathcal{R}\subset{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}={\overline{\triangle}}_{0}
is said to be
convex
if it satisfies the following condition: For any oriented path
(x_{1}\to x_{2}\to\cdots\to x_{l})
consisting of (vertical) arrows
in
{\overline{\triangle}}
, we have
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{l}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
if and only if
\{x_{1},x_{l}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
.

(3)

We say that a convex subset
\mathcal{R}\subset{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}
has a
upper bound
if there exists
\max(p\ |\ (i,p)\in\mathcal{R})
for each
i\in\triangle_{0}
.

(4)

For a convex subset
\mathcal{R}\subset{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}
, we set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{fr}}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\;|\;p=\min(k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\;|\;(i,k)\in\mathcal{R})\}

and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{ex}}\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{fr}}
. We denote by
{}^{\mathcal{R}}{\widetilde{\triangle}}
the valued quiver
associated to
{}^{\mathcal{R}}\widetilde{B}\mathbin{:=}(b_{(i,p),(j,s)})_{(i,p)\in\mathcal{R},(j,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{ex}}}
.

(5)

For a height function
\xi
on
\triangle
, let
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\widetilde{B}\mathbin{:=}(b_{(i,p),(j,s)})_{(i,p),(j,s)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.60275pt{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}}
and denote by
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{\triangle}}
the valued quiver associated to
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\widetilde{B}
, where

\displaystyle{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\in{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}\ |\ p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}.

Note that
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
is a convex subset of
{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}
for any height function
\xi
on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}
.

3.
t
-characters of quantum loop algebra and virtual Grothendieck rings

In this section, we first review the important properties of
t
-characters of finite-dimensional representations over quantum loop algebra briefly
(see
[
17
,
15
,
58
,
25
,
27
]
for more details).
Then we recall the virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})

for any finite type
\mathfrak{g}
(see
[
14
,
48
]
for non-simply-laced types).

3.1.
Quantum loop algebras

Let
t
be an indeterminate.
We denote by
\Bbbk\mathbin{:=}\overline{\mathbb{Q}(t)}
the algebraic closure of the field
\mathbb{Q}(t)
inside
\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}(\hskip-1.29167pt(t^{1/m})\hskip-1.29167pt)
. Let
\mathbf{g}
be a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra of simply-laced type. Note that, in this case, we can identify
\mathsf{C}(q)
with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
by exchanging
q
with
t
.

Throughout this paper, we use
bold symbols
to emphasize that those symbols are of simply-laced finite types. We also use
\imath,\jmath
for indices in
I^{\mathbf{g}}
for the same purpose.

We denote by
U_{t}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{g})
the quantum loop algebra associated to
\mathbf{g}
, which
is the
\Bbbk
-algebra given by the set of infinite generators, called the Drinfeld generators,
subject to certain relations
[
10
,
1
]
.
The quantum loop algebra
U_{t}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{g})
is a quotient of the corresponding
(untwisted) quantum affine algebra
U_{t}^{\prime}(\widehat{\mathbf{g}})
and hence has a Hopf algebra structure.

3.2.
Finite dimensional modules and their
t
-characters

We denote by
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
the category of
finite-dimensional
U_{t}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{g})
-modules of type
\mathbf{1}
.
The category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear rigid non-braided monoidal category.
We say that
V
and
W

commute
if
V\otimes W\simeq W\otimes V

as
U_{t}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{g})
-modules. We denote by
K(\mathscr{C})
the Grothendieck ring of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
. Note that the set of simple objects in

K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
are parameterized by the set
(1+z\Bbbk[z])^{I^{\mathbf{g}}}
of
I^{\mathbf{g}}
-tuples of monic polynomials, which is called
Drinfeld polynomials.

In this paper, we usually consider the
skeleton
subcategory
{\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}}
of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
. The subcategory
{\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}}
contains every
prime
simple module in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
up to
parameter shifts
. To explain
{\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}}
, we need to consider
the Laurent polynomial
\mathcal{Y}
generated by the set of variables
\{Y^{\pm 1}_{\imath,p}\}_{(\imath,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}
. Let us denote by
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\mathcal{M}_{-}
) the set of all monomials (resp. dominant monomials and anti-dominant monomials)
of
\mathcal{Y}
. For a monomial
\mathbf{m}
in
\mathcal{Y}
, we write

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}=\prod_{(\imath,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}Y_{\imath,p}^{u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathbf{m}_{-}=\prod_{(\imath,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}Y_{\imath,p}^{-u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})}

with
u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
. For each
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we denote by
L(\mathbf{m})
the simple module in
\mathscr{C}
whose Drinfeld polynomial is
\big(\prod_{p}(1-q^{p})^{u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})}\big)_{\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}}}
. Then the subcategory
{\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}}
can be characterized by the Serre subcategory
of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
generated by
\{L(\mathbf{m})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
. Note that
{\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}}
is a monoidal rigid subcategory of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
.
In
[
17
]
, Frenkel-Reshetikhin proved that there exists an injective ring homomorphism

\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}:K({\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}})\to\mathcal{Y},

called
the
t
-character homomorphism
5
5

5

It is usually called the
q
-character homomorphism in the literature.
.
The existence of
\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}
tells us that the Grothendieck ring
K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
is commutative, even though
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
is not braided.

For an interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
,
\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
and
(\imath,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we set dominant monomials

(3.2)

\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[a,b]\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\prod_{(\imath,s)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0};\ s\in[a,b]}Y_{\imath,s}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}_{k,p}\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\prod_{s=0}^{k-1}Y_{\imath,p+2s},

and
\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}(a,b]
,
\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[a,b)
, and
\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}(a,b)
are defined similarly.

The simple module
L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])

(p\leqslant s)
is called a
Kirillov–Reshetikhin
(
KR
)
module
. When
p=s
and
(i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we call
L(Y_{\imath,p})
a
fundamental module
.
Note that the Grothendieck ring
K({\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}}})
is a polynomial ring in the isomorphism classes of the fundamental modules
L(Y_{\imath,p})

[
17
]
.

For
\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}},a\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, we set

A_{\imath,a}\mathbin{:=}Y_{\imath,at^{-1}}Y_{\imath,at}\prod_{\jmath\colon d(\imath,\jmath)=1}Y_{\jmath,a}^{-1}=Y_{\imath,at^{-1}}Y_{\imath,at}\prod_{\jmath\neq\imath}Y_{\jmath,a}^{\mathbf{c}_{\jmath,\imath}}.

Note that there is an ordering
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
on the set of monomials, called the
Nakajima order
, defined as follows:

(3.3)

\mathbf{m}\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
if and only if

\mathbf{m}^{-1}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
is a product of elements in
\{A_{\imath,a}\mid\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}},a\in\Bbbk^{\times}\}
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
16
,
15
]
)
.

For each dominant monomial
\mathbf{m}
, the monomials appearing in
\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}(L(\mathbf{m}))-\mathbf{m}

are strictly less that
\mathbf{m}
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
.

The
t
-characters of KR-modules satisfies a system of functional equations called
T
-systems:

Theorem 3.2

(
[
58
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

(
See also
[
27
, Theorem 3.4]
.
)

For each
(i,p),(i,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathbf{g}}_{0}
with
p\leqslant s
, we have

(3.4)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s))\bigr)\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}(p,s])\bigr)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])\bigr)\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}(p,s))+\prod_{\jmath:\;d(\imath,\jmath)=1}\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\jmath)}(p,s)).

Let
\xi
be a height function on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}^{\mathbf{g}}
.
We denote by
{{}^{\xi}\mathcal{M}}_{+}
the set of all dominant monomials
in the variables
Y_{\imath,p}
’s for
(\imath,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.

Definition 3.3
.

We define the subcategory
\mathscr{C}^{\xi}_{\mathbf{g}}
as the Serre subcategory of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
such that

{\rm Irr}\;\mathscr{C}^{\xi}_{\mathbf{g}}=\{L(\mathbf{m})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in{{}^{\xi}\mathcal{M}}_{+}\}
.

Since
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
is a convex subset of
{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we have
the following proposition:

Proposition 3.4
.

The category
\mathscr{C}^{\xi}_{\mathbf{g}}
is a monoidal subcategory of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
.

Proof.

This assertion follows from the same argument of the proof of
[
30
, Proposition 3.10]
.
∎

3.3.
Truncation

We denote by

{\mathcal{Y}^{\xi}}
the Laurent polynomial ring generated by
Y_{\imath,p}
’s for
(\imath,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.
We define a linear map
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:\mathcal{Y}\to{\mathcal{Y}^{\xi}}
by sending
the monomials which contain some
Y_{\imath,p}
with
(\imath,p)\not\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}
to zero and by keeping all the other terms.

Proposition 3.5
.

For a height function
\xi
, the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-linear map
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:K(\mathscr{C}^{\xi}_{\mathbf{g}})\to{\mathcal{Y}^{\xi}}
given by

\displaystyle[V]\mapsto{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}(V)\mathbin{:=}((\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}\circ\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}})(V)

gives an injective ring homomorphism
K(\mathscr{C}^{\xi}_{\mathbf{g}})\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}^{\xi}}
.

Proof.

We can prove the assertion in the same way as in the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 6.1]
.
∎

3.4.
(Virtual) Grothendieck rings

Recall that when
\mathbf{g}
is of simply-laced finite type, the
t
-character homomorphism
\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}
is an injection from
K(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})
into
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{g}}
.
Thus we can identify
K(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})
with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})\mathbin{:=}\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}\bigl(K(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})\bigr).

We call
\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})
the Grothendieck ring of type
\mathbf{g}
also.

Proposition 3.6

(
[
15
, Corollary 5.7]
)
.

When
\mathbf{g}
is of simply-laced type, we have

\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})=\bigcap_{\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}}}\left(\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[Y^{\pm 1}_{\jmath,l}\ |\ (\jmath,l)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathbf{g}}_{0},\jmath\neq\imath]\otimes\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[Y_{\imath,l}(1+A_{\imath,l+1}^{-1})\ |\ (\imath,l)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathbf{g}}_{0}]\right)\subsetneq\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{g}}.

Now we move on to non-simply-laced finite types. For
\mathsf{g}
associated with
(\mathbf{g},\sigma)
in (
2.4
), we consider the Laurent polynomial ring
defined as follows: We first set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{g}}\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[X_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}^{\mathsf{g}}].

Then there exists a surjective ring homomorphism

(3.5)

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}:\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{g}}\xrightarrow{\,\hskip 6.02777pt\,}\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{g}}\qquad\text{sending}\qquad Y_{\sigma^{k}(\imath),p}\longmapsto X_{\overline{\imath},p}

for any
(\imath,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathbf{g}}_{0}
and
0\leqslant k<|\sigma|
(see Convention
1
). Finally, we set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})\mathbin{:=}\overline{\sigma}(\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g}))

and call it the
virtual Grothendieck ring
of type
\mathsf{g}
.
We call
\overline{\sigma}(L(\mathbf{m}))
the
folded
t
-character
of
L(\mathbf{m})
.

Now we would like to unify the expression for
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
for
any
finite type
\mathfrak{g}
by replacing variables
Y_{i,p}
’s with
X_{i,p}
’s.
Let
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{g}}
be the Laurent polynomial ring

\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[X_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}]
. For
(i,p+1)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}^{\mathfrak{g}}
, we set

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}X_{i,p-1}X_{i,p+1}\prod_{j\colon d(i,j)=1}X_{j,p}^{\mathsf{c}_{j,i}}.\end{split}

Definition 3.7
.

[
14
, §3.4]
We define the commutative ring

(3.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})=\bigcap_{i\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}}\left(\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[X^{\pm 1}_{j,l}\ |\ (j,l)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},j\neq i]\otimes\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[X_{i,l}(1+B_{i,l+1}^{-1})\ |\ (i,l)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}]\right)\subsetneq\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{g}}.\end{split}

Remark 3.8
.

Even though, we unify the expression for
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
by using
X_{i,p}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
B_{i,p}
, we sometimes use
Y_{\imath,p}
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
A_{\imath,p}
to emphasize that they are associated with
\mathbf{g}
of simply-laced finite type.

Theorem 3.9

(
[
14
, Proposition 3.3, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

(1)

Every element of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
is characterized by the multiplicities of the dominant monomials contained in it.

(2)

For each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there is a unique element
F(m)
of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
such that

m
is the unique dominant monomial of
F(m)
with its coefficient
1
. Therefore we have a
basis
\{F(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\mathfrak{g}}\}
of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})

parameterized by dominant monomials
m
.

(3)

For each pair
(\mathbf{g},\mathsf{g})
obtained via
\sigma
, the map
\overline{\sigma}
induces a surjective ring homomorphism from
\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})
to
\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
.

An
\mathcal{X}
-monomial
\mathbf{m}
is said to be
right-negative
if the factors
X_{j,l}
appearing in
m
, for which
l
is maximal, have negative powers.

Corollary 3.10
.

For each pair
(\mathbf{g},\mathsf{g})
obtained via
\sigma
and
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\mathbf{g}}
, assume that

(3.8)

every monomial in
F(\mathbf{m})-\mathbf{m}
is right-negative.

Then
\overline{\sigma}(F(\mathbf{m}))=F\bigl(\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m})\bigr)\in\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.9

3
and (
3.8
),
\overline{\sigma}(F(\mathbf{m}))
is an element in
\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
containing the unique dominant monomial
\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m})
.
Thus our assertion follows.
∎

Example 3.11
.

For finite
A_{5}
-type,
\overline{\sigma}(F(Y_{4,-2}Y_{2,0}))
does not coincide with

F(X_{2,-2}X_{2,0})
of finite type
C_{3}
, since
\overline{\sigma}(F(Y_{4,-2}Y_{2,0}))
does not satisfies (
3.8
). More precisely,

F(Y_{4,-2}Y_{2,0})
contains
Y_{3,-1}Y_{5,-1}Y_{2,0}Y_{4,0}^{-1}
. On the other hand,
\overline{\sigma}(F(Y_{2,-2}Y_{2,0}))=F(X_{2,-2}X_{2,0})
.

Note that if
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}
with
\mathbf{m}\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
, then we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m})\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{g}}.

It is proved in
[
15
,
27
]
that, for
\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s]\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\mathbf{g}}
,
F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
satisfies the condition in (
3.8
) and

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])=\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}(L(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])).

Thus we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}(F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))=F(m^{(i)}[p,s])

and (
3.4
) is changed into the following form:
For any finite type
\mathfrak{g}
and
(i,p),(i,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
with
p\leqslant s
, we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle F\bigl(m^{(i)}[p,s)\bigr)F\bigl(m^{(i)}(p,s]\bigr)=F\bigl(m^{(i)}[p,s]\bigr)F\bigl(m^{(i)}(p,s)\bigr)+\hskip-6.45831pt\prod_{j;\;d(i,j)=1}\hskip-6.45831ptF\bigl(m^{(j)}(p,s)\bigr)^{-\mathsf{c}_{j,i}}.

We call (
3.11
) the
folded
T
-systems
.

Definition 3.12
.

(1)

For a height function
\xi
on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}^{\mathbf{g}}
of simply-laced finite type, we set

{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})\mathbin{:=}{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}(K({\mathscr{C}^{\xi}})).

(2)

For a height function
\xi
on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}^{\mathsf{g}}
of non-simply-laced finite type, we set

\displaystyle{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})\mathbin{:=}\overline{\sigma}\bigl({{}^{\underline{\xi}}}\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})\bigr),

where
{\underline{\xi}}
is the
\sigma
-fixed height function on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}^{\mathbf{g}}
such that

{\underline{\xi}}_{\sigma^{k}(\imath)}=\xi_{{\overline{\imath}}}\qquad\text{ for any }0\leqslant k<|\sigma|\text{ and }\imath\in\sigma^{-1}({\overline{\imath}}).

We call
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
the
truncated virtual Grothendieck ring

and
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\overline{\chi}_{t}(\mathbf{m})
the
truncated folded
t
-character
of
L(\mathbf{m})
with respect to
\xi
, defined as below:

\textstyle{K({\mathscr{C}^{\underline{\xi}}}^{\mathbf{g}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.60275pt\overline{\chi}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{{{}^{\underline{\xi}}}\chi_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{${\scriptstyle{t}}$}}}
\textstyle{{{}^{\underline{\xi}}}\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\sigma}}
\textstyle{{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})}

Remark 3.13
.

Let
G
be a simply-connected complex Lie group associated with
\mathsf{g}
of
non-simply-laced type
.
In
[
14
]
, the authors formulate (conjectural)
folded integrable models
of
\mathsf{g}
corresponding to
folded Bethe Ansatz equations
.
Then
\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
, denoted by
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{-}(\mathsf{g})
in
[
14
]
6
6

6

In our introduction, we use
\mathcal{K}^{-}(\mathsf{g})
instead.
, plays the role of describing the spectra of the transfer-matrix
t_{V}(z,u)
with a finite-dimensional
U_{t}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{g})
-module
V
in the folded integrable model, as in the role of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathbf{g})\simeq K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}^{0})
in the integrable models for simply-laced types (cf.
[
13
,
14
]
for more details).
We remark that our main interest is to study the structure of the
quantization of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
introduced independently in
[
48
]
with other motivations related to
canonical basis
and
quantum cluster algebra structure
.
In contrast, the authors of
[
14
]
mainly focus on a study of the folded integrable models associated with
\mathsf{g}
.
It would be interesting to find connections between our results and those in
[
14
]
.

4.
Quantization

In this section, we quantize the Laurent polynomial ring
\mathcal{X}
with the resulting ring denoted by
\mathcal{X}_{q}
, via the inverse matrix
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}(t)
of (
2.9
) associated with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
following
[
48
]
(see also
[
14
]
), and define its subalgebra
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
that is regarded as a quantization of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
.

4.1.
Quantum torus

Let
q
be an indeterminate.
Let us recall that
\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(u)
(
u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
) in (
2.10
) and the even function
\widetilde{\eta}_{i,j}:\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
defined in (
2.11
).

Definition 4.1

(
[
59
,
66
,
25
,
48
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}_{q},*)
be the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-algebra with the
generators
\{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\;|\;(i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}\}

with the defining relations

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}=\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}=1
and

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{j,s}=q^{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)}\widetilde{X}_{j,s}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
,
where
(i,p)
,
(j,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
and

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)\mathbin{:=}\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(p-s-1)-\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(s-p-1)-\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(p-s+1)+\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(s-p+1).

We call
\mathcal{X}_{q}
the
quantum torus associated with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
(see Definition
7.1
below).

Remark 4.2
.

For simply-laced finite types,
the quantum torus
\mathcal{X}_{q}
was already defined in
[
59
,
66
,
25
]
, whereas for non-simply-laced finite types, it is introduced in
[
48
]
very recently.

Note that since
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}(t)
is symmetric,

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)=\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(j,p;i,s)=-\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,s;j,p)=-\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(j,s;i,p),

and it follows from Lemma
2.2
that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)=\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(p-s-1)-\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}(p-s+1)\qquad\text{ if }p>s.

Moreover, for
p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
and
i,j\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}
such that
(i,p),(j,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, Lemma
2.2
tells that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{j,p}=\widetilde{X}_{j,p}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}.

By specializing
q
at
1
, the quantum torus
\mathcal{X}_{q}
recovers the commutative
Laurent polynomial ring
\mathcal{X}
, while
\mathcal{X}_{q}
is non-commutative;
i.e., there exists a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra homomorphism
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}:\mathcal{X}_{q}\to\mathcal{X}
given by

q^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto 1
and
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\mapsto X_{i,p}
.

We say that
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
is a
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
if it is a product of the generators
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm 1}
and
q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}
.
For a
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we set
u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\mathbin{:=}u_{i,p}\bigl({\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\bigr)
(see (
3.1
)).
An
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
is said to be
right-negative
if
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})
is right-negative.
Note that a product of right negative
\mathcal{X}
-monomials (resp.
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials) is right negative.
A
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
is called
dominant
if

{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})
is dominant. Moreover, for
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we define

\widetilde{m}\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime}).

For
i\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}
, we call
\mathcal{X}
-monomial
m
(resp.
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
)
i
-dominant
if
u_{i,p}(m)\geqslant 0
(resp.
u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\geqslant 0
) for all
p
such that
(i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.
For
J\subset{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}
, we call
\mathcal{X}
-monomial
m
(resp.
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
)
J
-dominant
if
m
(resp.
\widetilde{m}
) is
j
-dominant for all
j\in J
.
For monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we define

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mathbin{:=}\sum_{(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}}u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})u_{j,s}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s).

There exists the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra anti-involution
\overline{(\cdot)}
on
\mathcal{X}_{q}
(
[
25
,
48
]
) given by

(4.5)

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto q^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\mapsto q_{i}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}.

Thus, for any
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, there exists a unique
r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that

q^{r}\widetilde{m}
is
\overline{(\cdot)}
-invariant.
A monomial of this form is called
bar-invariant
and denoted by
\underline{\widetilde{m}}
.
For an example,

\mathsf{X}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}q^{\frac{d_{i}}{2}}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
is bar-invariant.

More generally, for a family
\left(u_{i,p}\ \left|\ (i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}\right.\right)
of integers with finitely many non-zero components, the expression

(4.6)

\begin{split}q^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(i,p)<(j,s)}u_{i,p}u_{j,s}\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(j,s;i,p)}\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{(i,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}^{\to}\mathsf{X}_{i,p}^{u_{i,p}}\end{split}

does not depend on the choice of an ordering on
{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
and is bar-invariant.

Remark 4.3
.

Note that the relations in Definition
4.1
do not change when we replace
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
with
\mathsf{X}_{i,p}
, and

\underline{\widetilde{m}}
depends only on
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})
. Therefore, for every monomial
m
in
\mathcal{X}
, we denote by
\underline{m}
the bar-invariant monomial in
\mathcal{X}_{q}

corresponding to
m
.
Also the notation
Y_{i,p}
of
(\mathbf{Y}_{t},*)
in
[
29
, Section 3]
corresponds to
\mathsf{X}_{i,p}
, the bar-invariant monomial, in this paper.

For
(i,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we set

(4.7)

\widetilde{B}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}\underline{B_{i,p}}\ \in\mathcal{X}_{q}.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{q}^{-}
be the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by
\widetilde{B}_{i,p}^{-1}
’s for
(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
, we denote by
\mathbf{B}_{q}^{-k}
the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-span of the monomials
\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits^{\to}_{1\leqslant s\leqslant k}\widetilde{B}_{i_{s},p_{s}}^{-1}
.

For bar-invariant
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials
\underline{m_{1}}
and
\underline{m_{2}}
, we set
\underline{m_{1}}\cdot\underline{m_{2}}\mathbin{:=}\underline{m_{1}m_{2}}
, and for

\underline{m}_{k}

(k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1})
, we set

(4.8)

\prod_{k}\underline{m_{k}}\mathbin{:=}\underline{\prod_{k}m_{k}}.

Definition 4.5

(cf.
[
18
, Definition 5.5]
)
.

For a subset
\mathsf{S}\subset{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we denote by
{}^{\mathsf{S}}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{q}

the quantum subtorus of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by
\widetilde{X}^{\pm 1}_{i,p}
for
(i,p)\in\mathsf{S}\subset{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.
In particular, for a height function
\xi
on
{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}
, we denote by
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathcal{X}_{q}
the quantum subtorus generated by
\widetilde{X}^{\pm 1}_{i,p}
for
(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.

Proposition 4.6

(
[
48
, Proposition 5.7]
)
.

For
i,j\in I
and
p,s,t,u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
with
(i,p),(j,s+1),(i,t+1),(j,u+1)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
, we have

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{B}_{j,s}^{-1}=q^{\,{\mspace{1mu}\beta}(i,p;j,s)}\,\widetilde{B}_{j,s}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{B}^{-1}_{i,t}*\widetilde{B}_{j,u}^{-1}=q^{\,{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}(i,t;j,u)}\,\widetilde{B}_{j,u}^{-1}*\widetilde{B}_{i,t}^{-1}.

Here,

(4.9)

\displaystyle{\mspace{1mu}\beta}(i,p;j,s)

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}(-\delta_{p-s,1}+\delta_{p-s,-1})({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}),

(4.10)

\displaystyle{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}(i,t;j,u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pm({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})&\text{if $(i,t)=(j,u\pm 2)$,}\\
\pm 2({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})&\text{if $d(i,j)=1$ and $t=u\pm 1$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

4.2.
Quantization
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})

We briefly recall the construction of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, defined in
[
59
,
66
,
25
,
48
]
, by mainly following the argument in
[
24
,
25
]
.
For each
i\in I
, we define the free
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-left module

(4.11)

{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}\mathbin{:=}\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{r:\;(i,r)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}\mathcal{X}_{q}{\bm{\cdot}}\widetilde{s}_{i,r}

whose basis elements are denoted by
\widetilde{s}_{i,r}
.
We also regard
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
as a
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-bimodule by defining right
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-module action
{\bm{\cdot}}
as follows:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{i,r}\!{\bm{\cdot}}\widetilde{m}=q_{i}^{-2u_{i,r}(\widetilde{m})}\widetilde{m}{\bm{\cdot}}\widetilde{s}_{i,r},

where
\widetilde{m}
is an
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial (see Remark
4.11
, cf.
[
25
, Lemma 4.6]
).
Let
\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
be the quotient of
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
by the
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-submodule generated by the elements

(4.13)

\widetilde{B}_{i,r+1}\;\widetilde{s}_{i,r}-q_{i}\widetilde{s}_{i,r+2}\quad\text{ for }(i,r)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}_{0}.

By following arguments in
[
25
, Proposotion 4.8]
and
[
5
, Lemma 4.3.1]
, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 4.7
.

For each
l
with
(i,l)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
,
the
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-left module
\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
is free over any
\{\widetilde{s}_{i,r_{0}}\}
, where
(i,r_{0})\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
.

For all
i\in I
, we define

(4.14)

S_{i,q}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.95818pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.95818pt\raise 105.55086pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.55086pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{i,q}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where each map is defined as follows (recall (
4.11
) for definition of
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
):

(1)

the map
\widetilde{S}_{i,q}
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{i,q}(\widetilde{m})=\frac{1}{q_{i}^{-2}-1}\sum_{r:\;(i,r)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}\left[\widetilde{s}_{i,r},\,\widetilde{m}\right]

for an
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
, where
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
is regarded as the
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-bimodule,

(2)

the map from
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
to
\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
, denoted by an double-headed arrow, is the surjective map sending an element of
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
to its image in
\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
(recall (
4.13
)).

By direct computation, we have the following:

Proposition 4.8
.

The map
S_{i,q}
is a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear map and derivation with respect to
*
, that is,

(4.15)

\displaystyle S_{i,q}(\widetilde{m}_{1}*\widetilde{m}_{2})=\widetilde{m}_{1}{\bm{\cdot}}S_{i,q}(\widetilde{m}_{2})+S_{i,q}(\widetilde{m}_{1}){\bm{\cdot}}\widetilde{m}_{2},

where the
{\bm{\cdot}}
indicates the
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-bimodule actions of
\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
induced from
{}^{L}\hskip-1.29167pt\mathcal{X}_{i,q}
.

Definition 4.9
.

For
i\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})

the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by

\widetilde{X}_{i,l}*(1+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{B}_{i,l+1}^{-1})\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \widetilde{X}_{j,s}^{\pm 1}\quad\text{ for }j\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}\setminus\{i\}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ (i,l),(j,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}.

By using the same arguments as in
[
15
,
24
,
25
]
, we have

(4.16)

\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})={\rm Ker}(S_{i,q}).

Therefore, we call
S_{i,q}

the
i
-th
q
-screening operator
with respect to
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Definition 4.10
.

[
48
]

We set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathbin{:=}\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})

and call it the
quantum virtual Grothendieck ring associated to
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
.

Remark 4.11
.

Using the fact that
S_{i,q}
is a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-linear derivation (or by its definition with (
4.12
)), one can check that

S_{i,q}(\widetilde{X}_{j,l}^{-1})=-\delta_{i,j}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{s}_{i,l}
.
Then it follows from the definition of
S_{i,q}
, (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{i,q}(\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l-1})&=(-\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1})\widetilde{s}_{i,l}+(q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l-1})\widetilde{s}_{i,l-2}=0.\end{split}

In fact,
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})
is realized as the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by
\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}^{-1}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l-1}

and
\widetilde{X}_{j,s}^{\pm 1}
for
j\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}\setminus\{i\}
and
(i,l),(j,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
(cf. (
4.16
)).

Remark 4.12
.

Since the following diagram commutes (cf.
[
25
]
)

(4.17)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.27277pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.95818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.25706pt\raise 6.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.40556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{S_{i,q}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.95818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 68.46294pt\raise-9.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.46294pt\raise-12.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-19.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.001pt\raise-25.22498pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{S_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.0483pt\raise-19.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 60.0483pt\raise-19.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{i}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

where
S_{i}
is the
i
-th screening operator with respect to
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
,
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}\bigl(\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{K}(\mathfrak{g})
.
However, the opposite inclusion is not trivial (for non-simply-laced types).
We resolve this issue in the next section.

5.
Bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and Kazhdan–Lusztig analogues

Let
(\mathbf{g},\mathsf{g})
be a pair in (
2.4
).
It is known in
[
57
,
56
]
(see also
[
25
]
) that the basis
\mathbf{F}_{q}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})

with properties (
5.1
) below can be constructed algorithmically by using a deformed Frenkel–Mukhin (FM for short) algorithm (cf.
[
15
]
) with respect to
\mathsf{C}(q)
(so-called
t
-algorithm
[
25
]
).
This basis enables us to construct other important bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})
(see (
5.6
), Theorem
5.6
). In the second part of this section, we will construct a basis
\mathsf{F}_{q}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
by a deformed FM-algorithm with respect to
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
, and verify that it has similar properties to (
5.1
) by following the framework in
[
25
]
.
Moreover, we also construct other bases
\mathsf{E}_{q}
and
\mathsf{L}_{q}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
from the basis
\mathsf{F}_{q}
in the spirit of
[
57
,
25
]
in which they studied analogues of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
[
49
]
(see Theorem
5.27
, Remarks
5.7
and
5.28
).

5.1.
Bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})

Note that
\mathsf{C}(q)
coincides with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
for simply-laced finite types, when we replace
q
with
t
. Thus,

throughout this subsection, we switch the roles of
q
and
t
. Also, we use
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{-}
instead of
\mathbf{B}_{q}^{-}
.

This makes our notations more compatible with the literature where only simply-laced types are considered.

In
[
25
]
(cf.
[
57
,
59
]
),
the algorithm for constructing basis
\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbin{:=}\{F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
was proposed, so called
t
-algorithm
.
The structure and properties of the algorithm can be summarized as follows:

(5.1)

(1)

For each dominant
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomial
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
,
we construct an element
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
by adding monomials
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{A}_{t}^{-k}
in an inductive way as
k
increases from
0
. In the process, the coefficient for each monomial is also determined in an inductive way.

(2)

If there appears a unique
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
with the smallest
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}

satisfying

(a)

\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
is anti-dominant and
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{A}_{t}^{-k}
is generated in the performing step,

(b)

any monomial generated in the previous step is contained in
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{A}_{t}^{-s}

(0\leqslant s<k)
, not anti-dominant,
and strictly larger than
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
with respect to
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
,

then, the coefficient of
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}
is contained in
t^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}
.
Furthermore, the sum of all monomials with coefficients obtained from the steps so far, denoted by
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
, is contained in the kernel of
S_{\imath,t}
for all
\imath
. Hence

F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
is an element of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
and the
t
-algorithm terminates.

Furthermore, each
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})\in\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})\cap\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{A}_{t}^{-}
.

(2)

F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
is bar-invariant if
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
is bar-invariant.

(3)

Every monomial of
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})-\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
is strictly less than
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
with respect to
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
.

The
t
-algorithm might progress infinitely many times.
In fact,
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
was constructed in a completion of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
at first.
Interestingly, the property
1
in (
5.1
) is guaranteed once we prove

(5.2)

F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p})\in\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g}).

More precisely, (
5.2
) implies
\mathbf{E}_{t}:=\left\{E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\,|\,{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\right\}\subset\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
, where
E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
is given in (
5.6
).
Then it is known (e.g. see the proof of
[
25
, Proposition 6.3]
for more detail) that
\mathbf{E}_{t}
has the unit-triangular property with
\mathbf{F}_{t}
, that is,
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
can be written as a linear combination of elements in
\mathbf{E}_{t}\subset\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
,
so the proof for
1
in (
5.1
) is reduced to prove (
5.2
).
Then (
5.2
) is deduced from
[
57
,
59
]
.

Remark 5.1
.

Another characterization of
\mathfrak{K}_{\imath,t}(\mathbf{g})
in Remark
4.11
allows us to consider the lowest
\ell
-weight version of the
t
-algorithm,
that is, a
t
-deformation of
reversed
Frenkel–Mukhin algorithm which is an algorithm starting from the lowest
\ell
-weight monomial.
For instance, the formulas in
[
25
, Lemma 4.13]
can be re-formulated in terms of anti-dominant monomial with
\widetilde{A}_{\imath,k}
’s.
The reversed algorithm seems to be already known to experts in the theory of
q
-characters (e.g. see
[
15
]
,
[
55
]
).

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
be an anti-dominant (bar-invariant)
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomial. We denote by
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-})
the unique element of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})
generated by the
reversed

t
-algorithm (referred above) with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
. Then one can verify that
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-})
satisfies similar properties to (
5.1
) after modifying notations and terminologies associated with
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
.
For example, the property
3
in (
5.1
) associated with
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
is restated as every monomial appearing in
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-})-\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
is strictly
greater
than
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{-}
with respect to
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
. Throughout this section, we often refer to these properties.

Theorem 5.2
.

[
58
, Theorem 3.1]

[
27
, Theorem 4.1, Lemma 4.4]

For
(\imath,p),(\imath,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
with
p<s
, the element
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])\in\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
is of the form

\displaystyle F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])={\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]*(1+\widetilde{A}^{-1}_{\imath,s+1}*\chi),

where
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]\mathbin{:=}\underline{\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s]}
and

\chi
is a
(
non-commutative
)

\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-polynomial in
\widetilde{A}^{-1}_{\jmath,k+1}

(\jmath,k)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])=F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath^{*})}_{-}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}]),

where
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath^{*})}_{-}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}]\mathbin{:=}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath^{*})}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}])_{-}
.
Furthermore,
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
has the unique
(
anti-
)
dominant monomial
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]

(
resp.
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath^{*})}_{-}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}])
.

(2)

Each
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomial of
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])-{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]-{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath^{*})}_{-}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}]
is a product of
\widetilde{Y}_{\jmath,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p\leqslant u\leqslant s+\mathsf{h}
,
having at least one of it factors from
p<u<s+\mathsf{h}
,
and is right-negative.
In particular, each
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomial of
F_{t}(\underline{\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p}})-\underline{\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p}}-\underline{\widetilde{Y}_{\imath^{*},p+\mathsf{h}}}
is a product of
\widetilde{Y}_{\jmath,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p<u<p+\mathsf{h}
.

(3)

For
((\imath,p),(\jmath,p)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0},\;\jmath\neq\imath)
,
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
and
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath)}[p,s])
commute; i.e.,
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])*F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath)}[p,s])=F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath)}[p,s])*F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
.

It is well known that, for
r\in 2\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
and
\imath\in{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}_{0}
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{r}(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))=F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p+r,s+r]),

where
\mathsf{T}_{r}
is the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
sending
\widetilde{Y}_{i,p}
to
\widetilde{Y}_{i,p+r}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

[
25
, Theorem 5.11]

(1)

For every dominant
(
resp. anti-dominant
)
monomials
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{t}
,
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
is the
unique element in
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
is the unique
dominant
(
resp. anti-dominant
)
monomial of
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})
.

(2)

Every monomial appearing in
F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}})-\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
is strictly less
(
resp. strictly greater
)
than
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
with respect to
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
.

(3)

The set
\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbin{:=}\{F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
forms a
bar-invariant
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
.

Remark 5.4
.

We remark that an element in
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
is characterized by the multiplicities of its dominant monomials by Theorem
5.3
.
Then it yields that
{\rm ev}_{t=1}(F_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}))=F({\rm ev}_{t=1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}))
.

Example 5.5
.

We present
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
of type
D_{4}
(cf.
[
57
, Example 5.3.2]
) by organizing the monomials appearing in
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
as a directed graph
\Gamma(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
such that
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
is the sum of the monomials on the vertices of the directed graph, see (
5.5
).
Note that in this example, we write the
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomials according to the order given by

(5.4)

(\imath,p)<(\jmath,s)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\,p<s\,)\,\,\text{or}\,\,(\,p=s\,\,\text{and}\,\,\imath<\jmath\,).

We use the convention of
[
17
,
57
]
for the directed oriented graph
\Gamma(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
: For monomials
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{2}
, we use an colored directed edge
f(t)\;\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\overset{\imath,k}{\longrightarrow}g(t)\;\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{2}
if
{\operatorname{ev}}_{t=1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{2})={\operatorname{ev}}_{t=1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\widetilde{A}_{\imath,k}^{-1})
, where
f(t),g(t)\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.
Then the directed colored graphs
\Gamma(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
of
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
is given as below:

(5.5)

\widetilde{Y}_{2,0}
t^{2}\,\widetilde{Y}_{1,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{3,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{4,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{2,2}^{-1}
t\,\widetilde{Y}_{3,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{4,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{1,3}^{-1}
t\,\widetilde{Y}_{1,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{4,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{3,3}^{-1}
t\,\widetilde{Y}_{1,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{3,1}*\widetilde{Y}_{4,3}^{-1}

2,5

For a dominant monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\mathbf{g}}
, we set

(5.6)

E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\mathbin{:=}t^{a}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}^{\to}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{\imath\in I^{\mathbf{g}};(\imath,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p})^{u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})}\right)\right),

where
a
is an element in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}
appears in
E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
with the coefficient
1
.
Here

\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{\imath}F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p})^{u_{\imath,p}(\mathbf{m})}
is well-defined by Theorem
5.2

3
.
Note that
E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
contains
{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}
as its maximal monomial with respect to
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
. In particular, by Theorem
5.3
, we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})=F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})+\sum_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\mathbf{m}}C_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime})

with
C_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.
Note that the set
\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbin{:=}\{E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
also forms a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-basis since

(5.8)

\displaystyle\sharp\{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\ |\ \mathbf{m}^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\mathbf{m}\}<\infty\quad\text{ for each $\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}$}.

We call
\mathbf{E}_{t}
the
standard basis
of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
.

Note that
Y_{\imath,p}
is a minimal element in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
with respect to the partial order
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
. Thus (
5.7
) tells that

E_{t}(\underline{Y_{\imath,p}})=F_{t}(\underline{Y_{\imath,p}}).

Using the bases
\mathbf{F}_{t}
and
\mathbf{E}_{t}
, the third basis
\mathbf{L}_{\mathtt{t}}\mathbin{:=}\{L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
have been constructed in an inductive way using
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
such that

(5.9)

\displaystyle E_{t}(\underline{Y_{\imath,p}})=F_{t}(\underline{Y_{\imath,p}})=L_{t}(\underline{Y_{\imath,p}})

and
L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
for general
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
is characterized as
in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.6
.

[
59
]

(
see also
[
25
]
)

For a dominant monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}
, there exists a unique element
L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
in
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
such that

\overline{L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})}=L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
and

(5.10)

\begin{split}E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})=L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})+\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\mathbf{m}}P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime})\quad\text{ with $P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)\in t\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t]$. }\end{split}

We call
\mathbf{L}_{t}
the
canonical basis
of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
.

Remark 5.7
.

In a highly influential paper
[
49
]
, Kazhdan and Lusztig conjectured a realization of the composition multiplicities of Verma modules for
\mathfrak{g}
in terms of a certain class of polynomials defined by Iwahori–Hecke algebras, so-called
Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
(KL polynomials, for short).
The
Kazhdan–Lusztig conjecture
is that the specialization of the KL polynomials at
1
coincides with the composition multiplicities of Verma modules.
This is proved independently by Beilinson–Bernstein
[
2
,
3
]
and Brylinski–Kashiwara
[
6
]
.
Moreover, it is shown in
[
50
]
that the KL polynomials can be interpreted as the Poincaré polynomials for local intersection cohomology of Schubert varieties. This geometric interpretation gives the positivity of the KL polynomials.

A similar story has been developed in the representation theory of quantum loop algebras.
In
[
57
,
56
,
59
]
,
it is proved by Nakajima that the specialization of
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
at
t=1
gives the composition multiplicity of
L({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime})
in the standard module
E({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
. Furthermore,
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
coincides with the Poincaré polynomial of intersection cohomology of graded quiver varieties, which implies the positivity of
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
.
Consequently, the polynomials
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
’s may be viewed as analogs of KL polynomials.
It is worthwhile to remark that the recent development associated with
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
in the direction of quantum loop algebras beyond ADE-types, see
[
18
,
19
]
.

Theorem 5.8
.

[
59
]

(1)

For a dominant monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}
, every monomial in
L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
has a quantum positive coefficient;
that means, each coefficient of a monomial in
L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
contained in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.
In particular, we have
{\operatorname{ev}}_{t=1}(L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}))=\chi_{q}(L({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})).

(2)

For each monomial
\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s]
, we have
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])=L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
.

(3)

The coefficient
P_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}(t)
in (
5.10
) is actually contained in
t\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[t]
.

Remark 5.9
.

Let recapitulate the main points in this subsection.
From the
t
-algorithm, we obtain a basis
\{F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
. One crucial step is to prove that
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p})
is contained in
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
.
Then it is proved in
[
59
,
25
]
that there are frameworks for
constructing other two bases
\{E_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
and
\{L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}

of
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
.
In particular,
the basis
\{L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
is constructed using the other two bases through
the induction on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
via
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
, and there are uni-triangular transition maps (
5.7
) and (
5.10
)
between the three bases.

As
L_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
can be understood as a
t
-quantization of

L({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})
by Theorem
5.8

1
, the
T
-system among KR modules is also
t
-quantized as follows:

Theorem 5.10
.

[
29
, Proposition 5.6]

(
see also
[
58
, Section 4]
)

For
(\imath,p),(\imath,s)\in{\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-5.89856pt{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\hskip 3.69885pt}}}_{0}
with
p<s
, there exists an equation in
\mathfrak{K}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
:

(5.11)

\displaystyle L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s)\bigr)*L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}(p,s]\bigr)=t^{x}L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]\bigr)*L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}(p,s)\bigr)+t^{y}\hskip-4.30554pt\prod_{\jmath;\;d(\imath,\jmath)=1}\hskip-4.30554ptL_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath)}(p,s)\bigr),

where
L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath)}(p,s))
and
L_{t}\bigl({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\jmath^{\prime})}(p,s))

(\jmath,\jmath^{\prime}\in I)
are pairwise commute and

y=\dfrac{\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{\imath,\imath}(2(s-p)+1)+\widetilde{\mathsf{b}}_{\imath,\imath}(2(s-p)-1)}{2}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad x=y-1.

5.2.
Bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})

Assume that
\mathsf{g}
is of
non-simply-laced finite type
.
Since
\mathsf{C}(q)
can not be identified with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
anymore,

\emph{wecomebacktotheconventionoftheprevioussections(nottheprevioussubsection).}

Let
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})
be a completion of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
defined by the method in
[
25
, Section 5.2]
.
By following the construction of
\{F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\ |\ \mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathbf{g}}_{+}\}
in
[
24
,
25
]
, we can establish an analog of the
t
-algorithm in
[
25
, Definition 5.19]
on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})
, called
q
-algorithm
under the setting of Section
4.2
.
Roughly speaking, the algorithm is given inductively by computing all possible quantized
i
-expansions (cf.
[
25
, Lemma 4.13]
) while determining “correct” coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
of resulting monomials
[
25
, Definition 5.19]
(cf.
[
15
, Section 5.5]
).

The
q
-algorithm employs the
t
-algorithm by considering the another quantization
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
of the Cartan matrix
\mathsf{C}
. Thus, in the computational view point,
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
and
q_{i}\widetilde{B}_{i,p}
in the
q
-algorithm play the roles of

\widetilde{Y}_{i,p}
and
t\widetilde{A}_{i,p}
in the
t
-algorithm:

(5.12)

\widetilde{Y}_{i,p}\,\,\longmapsto\,\,\widetilde{X}_{i,p},\qquad t\widetilde{A}_{i,p}\,\,\longmapsto\,\,q_{i}\widetilde{B}_{i,p}.

We say that

(1)

the
q
-algorithm is
well-defined for step
s
if the
k
-th coefficients defined as in
[
25
, Definition 5.19]
with (
5.12
) are verified for all
k\leqslant s
,

(2)

the
q
-algorithm
never fails
if it is well-defined for all steps.

When the
q
-algorithm never fails, it yields, for each dominant monomial
\widetilde{m}
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
,

(5.13)

\displaystyle F_{q}(\widetilde{m})\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})=\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathfrak{K}^{\infty}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g}),

containing
\widetilde{m}
as a unique dominant monomial in
F_{q}(\widetilde{m})
. Here,
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})
is the completion of
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g})
satisfying

\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})\cap\mathcal{X}_{q}=\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g})={\operatorname{Ker}}(S_{i,q})

(see Lemma
5.32
below for more detail).
It is straightforward to check that the
q
-algorithm is well-defined and never fails
by following the framework of
[
25
, Section 5.3]
.
Since the proof is quite parallel to
[
25
]
,
the details are left to the reader.
As a result, we have the following.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty,\,f}(\mathsf{g})
be the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-submodule of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})
generated by elements in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})
with finitely many dominant monomials.
Then the set

\left\{\,F_{q}(\widetilde{m})\,|\,\text{$\widetilde{m}$ is a dominant monomial in $\mathcal{X}_{q}$}\,\right\}

is a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty,\,f}(\mathsf{g})
.
Indeed,
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty,\,f}(\mathsf{g})
is a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}^{\infty}(\mathsf{g})

Instead of the rigorous proofs of the above results,
let us illustrate the
q
-algorithm in Example
5.12
below by using results and notations in the parts after the example, frequently.

Example 5.12
.

We consider
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})
for type
G_{2}
.
Let us briefly summarize the procedure of
q
-algorithm. For
\mathcal{X}
-monomials
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, we use an colored directed edge
m_{1}\overset{i,\,p}{\longrightarrow}m_{2}
if
m_{2}=m_{1}B_{i,p}^{-1}
.
For
\mathcal{X}
-monomials
m
and
m^{\prime}
, we say that
m^{\prime}
is generated from
m
if there exists a finite sequence
\left\{(i_{1},p_{1}),(i_{2},p_{2}),\cdots,(i_{\ell},p_{\ell})\right\}\subset I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that

\textstyle{m=m_{0}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\,i_{1},\,p_{1}}
\textstyle{\,m_{1}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2},\,p_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\ell-1},\,p_{\ell-1}\hskip 8.19447pt\,\,\,}
\textstyle{\,m_{\ell}=m^{\prime},}

where
m_{k}
is a
\mathcal{X}
-monomial of
E_{J}(m_{k-1})
defined in (
5.31
) for
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
for some
J\subset I
.
We collect all possible
\mathcal{X}
-monomials generated from
X_{2,5}
, and then enumerate them by

(5.14)

\dots<m_{v}<\dots<m_{0}=X_{2,5},

where
<
is a total order compatible with
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
at
q=1
.
Let
\widetilde{m}_{v}
be a
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial determined inductively from
F_{J,q}(\widetilde{m}_{u})\in\bigcap_{i\in J}\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g})
for some
u<v
and
J\subset I
, where
F_{J,q}(\widetilde{m}_{u})
contains
\widetilde{m}_{u}
as a unique
J
-dominant monomial.
Note that
\widetilde{m}_{v}
is uniquely determined up to a coefficient in
q^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}
.
For this reason, we fix an order (
5.4
) on spectral parameters to write them uniquely.

Put
J\subsetneq I
.
We denote by

\left(s(m_{v})(q)\right)_{v\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}
and

\left(s_{J}(m_{v})(q)\right)_{v\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}

sequences in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]^{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}
defined inductively as follows:

(5.15)

\begin{split}s_{J}(m_{v})(q)&=\sum_{u<v}\left(s(m_{u})(q)-s_{J}(m_{u})\right)c_{J}(q)(m_{v}),\\
s(m_{v})(q)&=\begin{cases}s_{J}(m_{v})(q)&\text{if $m_{v}$ is not $J$-dominant,}\\
0&\text{if $m_{v}$ is dominant,}\end{cases}\end{split}

where

s(m_{0})(q)=1
,
s_{J}(m_{0})(q)=0
and

c_{J}(q)(m_{v})
is a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-coefficient of
\widetilde{m}_{v}
in
F_{J,q}(\widetilde{m}_{u})
.
Here we assume that
F_{J,q}(\widetilde{m}_{u})=0
if
m_{u}
is not
J
-dominant, so
c_{J}(q)(m_{u})=0
in this case.
Note that the sequences
\left(s(m_{v})(q)\right)_{v\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}
and
\left(s_{J}(m_{v})(q)\right)_{v\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}}
are well-defined, and
s(m_{v})(q)
does not depend on the choice of
J\subsetneq I
(e.g. see Remark
5.13
).
Finally, we verify that the sum of all
s(\widetilde{m}_{v})(q)\,\widetilde{m}_{v}
for
v\geqslant 0

is in
\ker(S_{i,q})
for all
i\in I
(e.g. see (
5.16
)). In other words,

F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})=\sum_{v\geqslant 0}s(\widetilde{m}_{v})(q)\,\widetilde{m}_{v}\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})
.

We emphasize that the monomials in (
5.14
) might be infinitely countable, but non-zero
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-coefficients of them should be finite (see Proposition
5.16
). Thus, the formula on the right-hand side makes sense, and it is actually a finite sum.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\setminus\{0\}
, we use
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{n}
to denote
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{*n}
for simplicity.
In fact, one may observe that
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})\in{\rm ker}(S_{i,q})
for
i=1,2
(see the complete computation in
[
34
]
).
For example,
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})
is written as

(5.16)

\begin{split}F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})&=F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})+q^{3}F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,6}^{3}*\widetilde{X}_{2,7}^{-1})+(q^{-3}+q^{-1}+q)F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,6}*\widetilde{X}_{1,8}*\widetilde{X}_{2,9}^{-1})\\
&\quad+(q^{-3}+q^{3})F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,7}*\widetilde{X}_{2,9}^{-1})+q^{6}F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,8}^{3}*\widetilde{X}_{2,9}^{-2})+q^{-3}F_{1,q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,11}^{-1})\end{split}

which is clearly in
{\rm ker}(S_{1,q})
(recall Proposition
5.31
).
The case of
i=2
is similar.
In general, for a dominant
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
,
the
q
-algorithm allows us to write
F_{q}(\widetilde{m})
as a linear combination of
F_{i,q}(\,\cdot\,)
’s over
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
. This fact plays a crucial role in the construction of
F_{q}(\widetilde{m})
. We present that the directed colored graphs
\Gamma(X_{2,5})
and
\Gamma(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})
of
F(X_{2,5})\in\mathfrak{K}(\mathsf{g})
and
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,5})\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
are given as follows, respectively:

(5.17)

\begin{split}\qquad\quad\raisebox{156.0pt}{
\scalebox{0.5}{$\hbox to253.48pt{\vbox to591.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-51.77179pt\lower-580.62712pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Here
F(X_{2,5})
is obtained from
{\rm ev}_{t=1}(\mathsf{T}_{5}(F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})))
(see Example
5.5
for
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{2,0})
) by folding the
\mathcal{Y}
-monomials (recall Remark
5.4
).

Remark 5.13
.

Each
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
-coefficient determined by the
q
-algorithm does not depend on the choice of
J
, which can be proved as in
[
25
, Lemma 5.20]
.
Let us recall that
F(X_{{\overline{\imath}},p})
is obtained from
F(Y_{\imath,p})
by folding the monomials of
F(Y_{\imath,p})
via (
3.5
) (see Corollary
3.10
). However, we would like to emphasize that we do not know yet whether
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{{\overline{\imath}},p})
could be obtained directly from
F_{t}(\widetilde{Y}_{\imath,p})
by folding
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
-monomials with some modification of coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
.

Definition 5.14
.

For
f\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(f)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\{{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\ |\ \widetilde{m}\text{ is a monomial in $f$}\},

\displaystyle\;\;\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{M}}(f)\mathbin{:=}\{\underline{m}\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}(f)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{+}(f)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\{{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\ |\ \widetilde{m}\text{ is a dominant monomial in $f$}\},

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}(f)\mathbin{:=}\{\underline{m}\ |\ m\in\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}(f)\}.

For
P\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
, a monomial
m
in
P
is called
maximal monomial
(resp.
minimal monomial
) if its weight is not lower (resp. not higher) than any other monomial in
P
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

(cf.
[
15
, Lemma 5.6]
)
For
P\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
, any maximal
(
resp. minimal
)
monomial in
P
is dominant
(
resp. anti-dominant
)
.

Proof.

Let us first consider a maximal monomial in
P
, denoted by
\widetilde{m}
. Take
i\in I
.
By Definition
4.9
and Proposition
4.6
, we have

P\in\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g})=\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\widetilde{X}_{j,l}^{\pm 1}]_{(j,l)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0},\,j\neq i}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\widetilde{X}_{i,l}+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l+1}^{-1}]_{(i,l)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}.

Hence, the element
P
can be written in the following form:

P=\sum\widetilde{m}_{(1)}*\widetilde{p}_{(2)},

where
\widetilde{m}_{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\widetilde{X}_{j,l}]_{(j,l)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0},\,j\neq i}
are monomials and,

\widetilde{p}_{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}][\widetilde{X}_{i,l}+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l+1}^{-1}]_{(i,l)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}}
are of the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}_{(2)}=n\;c(q)\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits^{\to}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,l)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0},\\
\text{ finite }\end{subarray}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,l}+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,l}*\widetilde{B}_{i,l+1}^{-1})^{n_{i,l}}

for some
n_{i,l}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
and
c(q)\in q^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}
.
In particular, the maximal monomial
\widetilde{m}
is a monomial in
\widetilde{m}_{(1)}*\widetilde{p}_{(2)}
.
Since
X_{i,l}B_{i,l+1}^{-1}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}X_{i,l}

the monomial
\widetilde{m}
should be obtained from
\widetilde{m}_{(1)}
and
\widetilde{X}_{i,l}
’s, .
Otherwise, it contradicts the assumption that
m
is a maximal monomial.
Since
i\in I
is arbitrary and
P\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
, the maximal monomial
m
should be dominant.
In the case of minimal monomials, the proof is almost identical because of another characterization of
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathsf{g})
in Remark (
4.11
).∎

For an interval
[a,b]
,
i\in I
,
(i,t)\in{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
, we define

(5.18)

\displaystyle m^{(i)}[a,b]\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,p)\in\widetilde{{\triangle\hskip-2.77277pt\raisebox{0.54247pt}{{\scalebox{.38}{${\mathbf{f}}$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt}}_{0}\\
p\in[a,b]\end{subarray}}X_{i,p}\quad\text{ and }\quad m^{(i)}_{k,t}\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\prod_{s=0}^{k-1}X_{i,t+2s}.

We define
m^{(i)}(a,b]
,
m^{(i)}[a,b)
, and
m^{(i)}(a,b)
in a similar way.

The following proposition plays a crucial role in proving fundamental results established on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
.

Proposition 5.16
.

For
p<s
,
let
m^{(i)}[p,s]
be given such that
\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])=m^{(i)}[p,s]

(
i.e.
\overline{\imath}=i
)
.

(1)

For each
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s]))
,
there exists
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))
such that
\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})=m^{\prime}
.

(2)

We have
F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s])\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
.

Proof.

We will give a proof of Proposition
5.16
in Section
5.3
.
∎

Definition 5.17
.

We call an element of the form
F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s])
a
KR-polynomial
. In particular,
we call
F_{q}(X_{i,p})
a
fundamental polynomial
. We also call a monomial of the form
m^{(i)}[p,s]
a
KR-monomial
.

Corollary 5.18
.

For
p<s
,
let
m^{(i)}[p,s]
be such that
\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])=m^{(i)}[p,s]

(
i.e.
\overline{\imath}=i
)
.
Then we have

\overline{\sigma}\left(\mathcal{M}(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))\right)=\mathcal{M}(F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s])).

Proof.

The inclusion
\supset
follows from Proposition
5.16

1
.
Let us prove the opposite inclusion
\subset
.
Let
m\in\overline{\sigma}\left(\mathcal{M}(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))\right)
be an
\mathcal{X}
-monomial, where we write
m=\overline{\sigma}(\mathbf{m})
for some
\mathcal{Y}
-monomial
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s]))
.
We have seen

(5.19)

{\rm ev}_{t=1}\left(F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])\right)=F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])

(see Remark
5.4
), and then
the quantum positivity for
F_{t}({\underline{\mathbf{m}}}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
in Theorem
5.8
with (
5.19
) implies that all the coefficients of
F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
should be positive. In particular, the coefficient of
\mathbf{m}
in
F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])
is positive.
Since it follows from Corollary
3.10
and Theorem
5.2
that

(5.20)

\overline{\sigma}\left(F(\mathbf{m}^{(\imath)}[p,s])\right)=F(m^{(i)}[p,s]),

the
\mathcal{X}
-monomial
m
appears in
F(m^{(i)}[p,s])
with a positive coefficient. But, we have

{\rm ev}_{q=1}\left(F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s])\right)=F(m^{(i)}[p,s])

(see Corollary
5.24
), which implies that there exists a term
f(q)\underline{m}
in
F_{q}(\underline{m}^{(i)}[p,s])
such that
{\rm ev}_{q=1}(f(q)\underline{m})=f(1)m
is a term in
F(m^{(i)}[p,s])
with
f(1)>0
.
∎

Proposition 5.19
.

For each
(i,p)\in{\widetilde{\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\blacktriangle$}}}\hskip-8.61108pt\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{ {\color[rgb]{1,1,1} ${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\ }}_{0}
, we have

(1)

F_{q}(\mathsf{X}_{i,p})=F_{q}(\mathsf{X}^{-1}_{i^{*},p+\mathsf{h}})
contains only one anti-dominant monomial
\mathsf{X}^{-1}_{i^{*},p+\mathsf{h}}
.

(2)

All
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials of
F_{q}(\mathsf{X}_{i,p})-\mathsf{X}_{i,p}

-\mathsf{X}_{i,p+\mathsf{h}}^{-1}
are products of
\widetilde{X}_{j,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p<u

<p+\mathsf{h}
.

(3)

F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{i,p})
and
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{j,p})

((i,p),(j,p)\in{\widetilde{\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\blacktriangle$}}}\hskip-8.61108pt\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{\scalebox{.53}{ {\color[rgb]{1,1,1} ${\mathbf{f}}$}}}}\ }}_{0},\;j\neq i)
commute.

Proof.

Since
F_{q}(\mathsf{X}_{i,p})
is an element in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
, it contains an anti-dominant monomial by Lemma
5.15
. Then Theorem
5.2
and Proposition
5.16

tell that
F_{q}(\mathsf{X}_{i,p})
has the unique antidominant monomial
\mathsf{X}_{i^{*},p+\mathsf{h}}^{-1}
. Thus
1
follows.
By (
3.9
),
2
follows from
1
, Theorem
5.2

and Proposition
5.16
. Finally,
3
follows from the same argument as in
[
25
, Lemma 5.12 (iv)]
.
∎

Example 5.20
.

As in Example
5.12
,
one may compute the formula of
F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})
given by

\widetilde{X}_{1,10}+q^{2}\widetilde{X}_{2,11}\widetilde{X}_{1,12}^{-1}+q^{2}\widetilde{X}_{1,12}^{2}\widetilde{X}_{2,13}^{-1}+(q^{-1}+q)\widetilde{X}_{1,12}\widetilde{X}_{1,14}^{-1}+q^{3}\widetilde{X}_{2,13}\widetilde{X}_{1,14}^{-2}+\widetilde{X}_{1,14}\widetilde{X}_{2,15}^{-1}+q^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{1,16}^{-1}.

Then
F_{q}(\mathsf{X}_{1,10})=(\mathsf{X}_{1,10}*\widetilde{X}_{1,10}^{-1})F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})=q^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathsf{g})
is bar-invariant.
Note that
\widetilde{X}_{1,10}*\widetilde{X}_{2,10}=\widetilde{X}_{2,10}*\widetilde{X}_{1,10}

and there is no dominant
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})*F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,10}))
except for
\widetilde{X}_{1,10}*\widetilde{X}_{2,10}
(cf. Example
5.12
).
Hence we have

F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})*F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,10})=F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{2,10})*F_{q}(\widetilde{X}_{1,10})
.

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
